ParlaMint-DK_20190425101948	Thank you. After all, it is no more than four months since we last voted on a proposal like this in this Chamber. As I said at the time, I fully acknowledge the good intentions of the proposers of the proposal, because it goes without saying that all sexual acts must be voluntary. I do not have the imagination to imagine that we disagree on that in this Chamber. When I choose to speak today, this is because the proposal we are debating has followed the special committee procedure for dealing with motions for resolutions, which means that we have not had the opportunity to discuss it in this Chamber. I think that is a pity, because a great deal has happened over the last four months. That is why it is also important for me to stress that when the government votes against the resolution today, it does not mean that the proposal goes into oblivion. However, the motion for a resolution proposes that a bill on a consent-based rape clause should be tabled in this Assembly. That is simply not realistic. Nor is it realistic when the proposers do not make a concrete proposal as to what a new rape clause should look like. Everyone in this Chamber knows very well that there are a number of important legal considerations which must be taken into account in the formulation of a new rape clause, and that therefore requires proper preparatory work, a preparatory work which could simply not be achieved in this session, if all the parties in the Folketing so wished. The government, on the other hand, has given full impetus to the work. I have recently appointed a new President and a number of new members of the Criminal Code Council, and I have asked the Council, led by the Supreme Court, Thomas Rørdam as the first to carry out a thorough review of the penal code's rape clause and to make concrete proposals on how it can be clarified that sexual acts should be voluntary. For such an important provision must, of course, be made thoroughly and properly. However, the Criminal Code Council will have to complete its work in sufficient time for proposals to be made to amend the rape clause as early as the next session. So it is not in any way about dragging the work into a long process. It is a question of ensuring that a new rape clause is easily understood and applicable to citizens and to authorities at the same time. We owe it to the victims. And if there is any body capable of fulfilling this task, then it is the Criminal Code Council which has the absolute highest legal professionalism that can be patterned in this field of criminal law. I think it really is a bit too bad that it might appear that we in this House disagree on this point, because I think we actually agree. We agree that this has to be done thoroughly. We agree that sex should be based on volunteering, that goes without saying. And I hope, when the Criminal Law Council has had the opportunity to look at it and makes their proposals, that we can then, as a single Parliament, stand together on a new provision, but that is not just something you pull in a vending machine. This requires very thorough work by the highest legal experts. And I hope, despite the formal disagreement we have here, that at a later stage we will be united in this. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425102523	Thank you. We are dealing with the motion for a resolution called B 146 and, in order to put an end to the misunderstanding which tended to occur just now, it is therefore a question of the Folketing imposing on the government in this parliamentary assembly and then anyone can guess how long it lasts, or at least how long we sit here, to make proposals so that it is based on consent in terms of rape. Technically, this is not possible, and it is not really anymore. The Ministry of Justice has also confirmed this in response to question 3. But, having said that, technicalities should of course not prevent us from debating such an incredibly important subject as this one is, here in the Folketing. What this is basically about is implementing consent-based legislation in the field of rape. We do not have the exact wording. No one can really tell us which legal text is the basis for the formulation in question, which is vital for both the victim and the defendant. But we know that it must be consent-based. It is, and remains, the essential element that women must have consented in one way or another. Otherwise it's rape, I'm one hundred percent in agreement, it's Left one hundred percent in agreement. Whether there is a change in this and the extent to which it is a change in existing legislation, I am not going to be wise here, but I note that a great many believe that this is not the case today and that women are being raped to a large extent where they have not given a plausible consent. And I just have to say that, from the point of view of the Left, sex must be based on voluntaryness, and, if it does not already do so, it must be clearly stated in the law. And on that basis, I think it is excellent that we have now had the Criminal Law Council set up so that it can be made quite clear. In the Left, we want more rapists convicted. It could be your daughter that got hurt. We also want the innocent to go free. It could have been your son that got hurt. Finally, we want to maintain the usual assessment of proof, the usual rule of proof in the Code of Procedure, namely that it is the prosecution that has the burden of proof. And here it must be the burden of proof that women have not given their consent. It is not an easy task to formulate legislative proposals in this area, and this certainly cannot happen within the time frame we have set aside. Every lawyer and every judge knows that it is a very difficult assessment and that it is even more difficult to formulate a legislative text covering these matters. After all, it is not difficult to judge in cases where rape has taken place, where someone has been attacked on a dark path and has thus been mutilated both on body and soul. I do not think that is a problem for the judges in Denmark. On the other hand, what is causing problems is the practical case where two people are interested in an apartment; they have intercourse and the next day there is a police report. It's a claim against claim. There is no technical evidence of rape, which is why it is crucial that we get the Criminal Code to look at whether, in these situations, rape should be condemned and, if so, how it should be done. I hope we can formulate legislation that covers it, but I have not heard how. On the other hand, I have read that many judges are seriously afraid that so-called consent-based legislation will give women's behaviour a higher priority when judging in court, and I have to say that that would be deeply, deeply regrettable. So this is just another example of how important it is to get the Criminal Law Council across so that we get the best possible legislation in this area. I am confident to see that the Justice Minister has now set up the Criminal Code Council to look at this legislation that is incredibly important both for the victim and for the defendant. This requires thorough consideration, and therefore there are both city courts, national courts and Supreme Court judges and professors and other experts on the Criminal Law Council. I would like to see that, but I would not like us to go through a solution in this Assembly. That would be an insult to both the victim and the defendant. So, on this basis, the Left cannot support the proposal, but we can welcome the fact that we now have a revision of the legislation through the thorough examination of the Criminal Code Council.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425103005	Thank you, sir. Preben Bang Henriksen. There are no brief comments. And then the ride's come to sir. Rune Lund, Enhedslisten, De Red-Green.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425103014	Thank you. A single opposition has put forward this proposal to ensure that we have much more up-to-date legislation on rape. And on the part of Enhedslisten, we also want legislation based on consent, because it is basically about getting legislation that reflects what is right and wrong. For the rape legislation we have today means that it is the woman or the victim who is responsible for rejecting it. If she is completely passive, perhaps for fear or other reasons, it is legitimate for another person to have sex with her, even if she has not said yes in one way or another. In other words, the legislation says that people are, as a rule, entitled to your body unless you say yes, and not that you are entitled to your body as a starting point, unless you say yes. A consent-based legislation will mean that you have the responsibility to ensure that others are okay with what you do to them. You must not just have sex with another person without ensuring that they actually want it. Today you don't have to stop if you're in doubt, you can just keep going. With consent-based legislation, we make sure that if you are in doubt, you have to stop. And there is good reason to make that clear, because it is not only rapists who are freed from it but there are also women who never get reported abuse and there are abuses that are not categorized as what they are, namely abuse, even if they should be. We are pleased that the entire opposition is in the process. When the parliamentary assembly started in 2015, there were only four parties in the opposition that supported consent-based legislation in the field of rape, but fortunately the alternative has also been included. And it must also be noted that the government and the bourgeois parties have moved on this issue and the problem has been recognised. Both what the Prime Minister and the Minister for Justice say and, to that extent, what the Minister for Justice says up here from the platform means that the Criminal Law Council must now look at what kind of models can come into play, and that is what the parties behind the proposal here want, as it were. It is very good, and it is in an important area that we have seen a very important political movement. But I also think it is a pity that there cannot be a united Parliament today voting in favour of this proposal. Voting through this proposal does not mean that good legislation can subsequently be made; voting through this proposal by a unanimous Parliament would send out the strongest and clearest signal in relation to the desire to introduce legislation in the field of rape based on consent. At any rate, I am pleased that an overall opposition wants to send that signal very clearly.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425103507	Amendments have been tabled. Does anyone wish to speak? Mr. Finn Sørensen from the Enhedslisten... the Red Greens. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425103545	Thank you. The Enhedslisten has tabled a number of amendments to these two legislative proposals, and the aim of our amendments is to remove the deterioration of the unemployed, as we can see in the legislative proposals. Unfortunately, we think that the conciliation parties behind the employment policy at least behind these draft laws are suffering from something I would call a compulsion to do nothing good for the unemployed without the devil's violence and power at the same time reducing the conditions of the unemployed in some other areas. We would like to see this deterioration removed before we can vote in favour of the legislative proposals. We pointed this out at first reading, where we thoroughly reviewed this very large legal complex. We commended the good things in it; we criticised the deterioration in it. So I shall not repeat that here. When I take the floor now, it is because we have tabled a further amendment which has not been included in the report. It is a question of volunteering for the unemployed to take part in company practice. After all, the minister likes to say that enterprise-oriented action is the best way to get unemployed in ordinary employment. And that is right when you look at wage subsidies and the results of wage subsidies in private companies. There are fairly good results with it, it is about half who gets into ordinary employment immediately after a completed wage subsidy job. However, if we are talking about company practice, the Minister's own figures show that things look quite different. Only yesterday we received answers to a number of questions we have asked about this, and the answers confirm what we were told about a year ago and which we also had the Minister in consultation on then, namely that there is virtually no employment effect of company practice. The number in ordinary employment 1 month after the end of the internship is basically the same now as it has been since 2013, i.e. 12%, while the latest figure we received showed 111⁄2%. Nearly all other trainees are back on public services after completed work placements. This confirms our view that company practice is primarily a means of making life miserable for the unemployed either because they are ill and cannot work or because they are systematically used as free labour. It is an extensive displacement of ordinary jobs that takes place here. Every year, there are just over 140,000 people in company practice in private companies. With a very careful calculation, this corresponds to approx. 15,000 full-time jobs. We are fully aware that in the draft law the Minister proposes some tightening up of the purpose clause, so that internship should be more focused on ordinary employment and that is, of course, excellent. But we are not very impressed by the wording when you see that somewhere else in the same bill weakens the influence of the employees and trade unions on whether there is abuse in relation to company practices. In order to prevent abuse, we need to take a different approach; therefore, our proposal that it should be voluntary for the unemployed person to take part in company practice. If it is adopted, it will of course mean, as the law also has the obligation for the municipalities to offer the unemployed some offers, that the job centre will then have to offer the unemployed some other offers, namely counselling offers, qualification and training, and that, in the view of Enhedslisten, is also the way we should go. But unfortunately, the government wants to go the other way. After all, the minister does not hide the fact that he wants to press or force the municipalities to spend less resources on what the unemployed need: guidance, retraining and training, and spend more effort on delivering what the companies want, namely some free labour, and that will also be the result of this bill. This is not directly stated in the draft law. I would like to stress that this criticism should not be directed directly at the parties supporting it, but it is of course about the underlying economy, because the government and the Danish People's Party decided a few months ago to remove a billion kroner from the employment efforts of the municipalities. Then it happens that, at the same time, if you remove the obligations of the municipalities and set them freer, compared to what they will do for the unemployed, then it will be the cheapest possible solution that the municipalities choose, and that is the corporate approach. The municipalities are being pressured by the economy to offer what is cheapest and easiest for the municipality and which many companies, not least the large supermarket chains, demand. We think it is time we gave the unemployed some tools to protect themselves from it; therefore our amendment, which of course must also be adopted if we are to vote in favour of this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425110140	The debate is open and the first to speak is the Minister for Industry. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425110207	Thank you. It is a proposal from the Enhedslisten that is about setting up an expert group that, with the inspiration of public banks in other European countries, must come up with proposals and models for setting up a nationwide publicly operated bank. The government is not in favour of public banks being operated and therefore cannot support the proposal. There is nothing to suggest that the state should be able to find out how to run a bank, in our view. The State does not have those competences, and the State does not have to take over tasks that private companies already perform. I understand that the reason for the proposal is the cases of money laundering and dividend cases in the financial sector in recent times, cases which, of course, are completely unacceptable, but in our view these cases are not an argument for setting up state banks, as state banks are not necessarily a protection against economic crime. Trust in the financial sector must be ensured through regulation and by ensuring effective supervision, including the prevention of financial crime. That is why, of course, we are pleased that a short time ago we managed to reach agreement in the Folketing on an agreement that significantly strengthens the fight against financial crime. The Enhedslisten has asked several times during the parliamentary term why the party cannot be included in the financial conciliation group. This is the group of parties where we usually make legislation or at least make agreements on legislation for the financial sector. • The Enhedslisten has wondered whether there are any differences at all that make us have a financial conciliation group that is not part of the party. I think that a proposal like this illustrates the differences very well. There is a very big difference between Enhedslisten's visions for the financial sector and, at least, the government's visions for the financial sector, where we believe, first and foremost, that it should be driven by the market and not that the state should go in and take over the banking sector. This also applies to the next proposal, which we shall also be considering, from the Enhedslisten, where it is proposed directly that large banks should be split up. Nor is it a vision shared by the government. We want to crack down on financial crime, and I think we have done so with the agreements we have made, where we have lifted the penalties and where we strengthen supervision, because of course we do not accept that some people are condoning money laundering in the financial sector or other forms of financial crime. So we want to be tough on the financial crime that needs to be combated, but we do not want to fight the financial sector, nor do we want the state to take over the financial sector, and there I see quite a difference in the way that the government looks at the financial sector and the way that Enhedslisten views the financial sector.

If a bank goes down today, it is the shareholders who lose the money; if it were a state bank, it would be taxpayers who lost the money. I do not need to say anything more than that we are in principle opposed to the public having to conduct commercial banking, and that is why we do not support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425115245	Thank you very much, and thank you for the debate, which is, of course, the first of two debates we will have today, on the financial sector and on how we can get a financial sector which, perhaps to a little greater extent than the one we know today, works for the benefit of ordinary Danes, whether you are a citizen or in small businesses. Basically, this proposal is about giving citizens greater freedom. It is about creating more effective competition, and it is about ensuring a greater democratic impact on investment flows in our society. And I'll start with the first thing about free choice. In many other European countries, citizens are free to choose whether they want to have their banking business in an investor-owned bank, in other words a limited company, whether they want to have it in a cooperative bank or a savings bank, or whether they want to have a public banking connection. This is the case in Germany, where the majority of Germans have this kind of banking connection. We see it in Sweden and in France and many other places. I have, of course, experienced, especially after the scandals of recent years, that a great many citizens say: Why can't I have my account in a public banking offer? As I said, if we look into Europe, we can see that 10%. of the financial sector is 100% state owned. If you count the banks that have been bailed out, as they say, which are temporarily taken over by the state, then we are as much as 30 percent. As the SF's rapporteur mentioned earlier, we also had Girobank, who acted as a state bank, where you could keep your account. And that's kind of just what we want again. We do not want there to be just one bank offer and that it should be state. We want there to be a public banking offer in line with the other banking offers we know from other countries. One of the places where we obviously have a problem with free choice is in our municipalities. Following the scandals, a number of major municipalities have tried to move their bank connections away from Danske Bank or Nordea or some of the other banks that have been hit by scandals. But it's turned out to be very, very difficult. Firstly, because it seems that it requires a bank with a certain amount of money, not so many people are going in but partly because one gets the recommendation that only so-called SIFI banks, i.e. banks, have been given this state guarantee in the back against not being able to go bankrupt, which is really in that concept. So it forces a municipality like Svendborg to choose between either the money laundering bank on the one hand or the tax havens on the other. And that seems to me to be a democratic problem, that is to say, although a majority of citizens in a municipality actually want a different bank connection for their municipality, they are not actually able to choose it. A public banking offer could also be an answer to that. I mentioned earlier that the Social Democratic Mayor of the City of Faaborg-Midtfyn, Hans Stavnsager, in a chronicle, has proposed exactly that on the basis of his frustration at his experiences with the offers that were available. Another advantage in addition to this is that there would be better competition. After all, we have seen a lot of reports in recent years that show that competition in the Danish financial sector is, to put it mildly, not very well functioning. This is, of course, the case in the mortgage sector, where we have very large mastodons and very little competition, but it is also the case in the financial institutions where we see that the Danes are some of those who change the very least bank compared to many other countries. And that's because it's not that easy. I just had an experience with it myself. I'm redeploying a loan. I've had brfkredit for many years, and I've been kind of happy about that. It was, moreover, an old state mortgage fund set up by the general housing companies together with the state back in the 1950s. It's been taken over by Jyske Bank. The money from the sale is, moreover, a completely different story... ended up in a fund that five self-appointed people manage without any democratic influence, despite the fact that it was the general housing sector and the state that shot in the original money, and of course the borrowers. But we can take that another day. I would now like to have my loan changed, and there I am told that the prerequisite for that is that I should move not only me but also the people I live with to Jyske Bank, i.e. our entire banking business, all of them. It is, after all, one of these examples of, I think, a lack of competition in our sector which is deeply problematic. And I believe that the presence of a public banking offer could put pressure on private banking offers, as we see in a number of other countries. It is probably not by chance that it is in Denmark that we have seen such a sharp increase in fees compared to some of the countries where there is a stronger presence of public ownership, but also a stronger presence of consumer ownership, i.e. cooperative funds and savings banks. A further argument is about economic stability. That is what I said a short time ago. A lot of research has been done which shows that the problem with private, investor-owned banks is that they are acting very procyclically. That means that when things go well in the economy, they lend a lot of money. That's usually where we need them not to borrow so much money. And when things go bad in the economy, they close the box to small businesses. And that's where we needed them to actually start the loans, or at least kept their hands on some of these small businesses. There is therefore evidence that public banks and, moreover, consumer-owned banks are acting in a much more countercyclical manner. I think many small businessmen could have enjoyed that. I myself know people who simply closed their overdraft from one day to the next. Not because they did not have a well-functioning business, but because a financial crisis had hit the banks. The last argument is a little more high-handed, but it is about democratic influence. In this time when we need to move huge investments in a very short time in order to reorganise our economy in a green and sustainable direction, I think one can ask oneself whether the investment decisions taken in our society are something we dare leave to a relatively reduced group of investors and shareholders. Or whether it would make sense for us, as citizens, to be more able, through consumer-owned banks and as citizens through public financial institutions, to direct investment flows towards some of the investments that we need here and now. So that is the main argument as to why we think this proposal is important. There have been some arguments in the debate, and thank you for that. One argument has been whether the state has any competence at all for this. And it is clear that I do not imagine that neither I nor anyone else will be bank managers in a public bank. It is clear that there must be professional leadership, just as we know it from ATP or other public or semi-public institutions run professionally. And, of course, this should also be the case with a public bank, as is the case in other countries, but with a democratic opportunity to exert influence and a democratic framework around the bank. And first of all, with the quality that distinguishes them from the investor-owned banks, namely that it should rest in itself. It was not intended to deliver large annual profits to external shareholders. It has been said that we have a well-functioning market. I also mentioned this question, and I believe that a great many people will disagree with it. So, to conclude, I would like to thank you for an interesting debate. I think it is a good thing that these scandals have given rise to more fundamental and fundamental debates on what kind of financial sector we want. I can hear that we are not going to get this proposal adopted this time, but I hope that the debates we have had and are going to have will clear up some of the, I think, somewhat black-and-white and mythical discussions we have had. I would say that when I did this work myself on Enhedslisten's financial sector initiative, I was actually surprised at how much public participation there is in the European banking sector and how much research shows that it can actually be quite healthy for a financial sector to have that presence. I hope we can get this into the debate so that we can have a more thorough and enlightened debate on these issues in the future. But thank you for today's debate, at least for the time being.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425130000	We are opening negotiations with the Minister of Business. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425130030	Thank you. Then we have reached the second round, a little on the same ball street, a motion for a resolution from the Enhedslisten to set up a financial structural commission to examine the potential for and assess the advantages and disadvantages of a division of systemically important financial institutions, the so-called SIFIs, i.e. the large banks. The government does not want to split up the banks and cannot therefore support the proposal. As I said at the previous point, where Enhedslisten proposed to create a public bank, the government and Enhedslisten have very different visions for the financial sector. We in the government want to fight economic crime hard, but we do not want to fight the financial sector as such. We do not want the state to run banks, nor do we want to have banks split up. Of course, there are some challenges to having very large banks, which make up a very large part of the economy, because if they go bankrupt, there is a risk that they can pull down the entire economy. We are very aware of this, and that is why there are also a large number of requirements for the large banks, which are much stricter than the requirements for other banks. The SIFIs must meet tough liquidity and capital requirements. They are subject to a special so-called SIFI supplement and there are other requirements in the capital requirements legislation, and the SIFI supplement varies with the systemicity of institutions, i.e. their size. In addition, large institutions set a capital objective which reflects the institution's capital plans and management's assessment of capital needs. Danske Bank has a capital target for a solvency ratio of more than 20%, which is a large increase over the pre-financial crisis, with a solvency ratio of 13% at the end of 2008. Then I would also like to emphasise that we in Denmark have, of course, implemented the EU Crisis Management Directive. The main principle, as has also been the principle in Denmark since banking package III, is that it is the shareholders and creditors of the institutions who have to bear the losses in the event of a bank having problems. It's not the state that has to bear the losses. SIFIs should not be encouraged to risk too much in the expectation that the state will save them if things go wrong. One of the most important things is the requirements for the so-called write-down liabilities, the NEP requirements, and it is the SIFIs that have received the highest NEP requirements in order to address the systemic nature of institutions. Thus, NEPs are claims that are lost if a bank is going bankrupt. Danske Bank has received a NEP claim of 33 per cent. of the risk-weighted exposure amounts corresponding to approx. 235 billion kr. That's a lot of money. It is a big buffer to be able to take off if you are about to go bankrupt, which means that we consider the likelihood that we would get into a situation where it would be the state that would have to pay to hold hands under Danske Bank, for example, as very small. Before that, there would have been huge losses among shareholders and creditors, and we expect this to be a strong incentive for both shareholders and creditors to keep the institutions on the right path and that they do not start taking a lot of risks in anticipation of the state then coming and saving them. The FSA's data show that the loss risk associated with Danish SIFI's loans is significantly lower than the risk associated with the loans of the small and medium-sized institutions, and this is of course remarkable. This means that there are no indications that the SIFIs base credit on an expectation of state rescue measures. If there were, it could be expected that there would then be a greater risk-taking in the large banks than in the small banks, and that is not the case according to the FSA's data. So I would like to conclude by saying that the separation of banks will create disadvantages for customers. Today, customers benefit from so-called full customer benefits. It can, for example, be done. be in the form of discounts, and it can be the service of large companies, where you get service in one bank on several different activities that the bank solves for you. This would then not be possible if the banks were to split up and only be allowed to take care of certain activities and there would then be other companies that took care of other activities. Overall, therefore, it is the view of the government that we have taken good care of the systemicity that the large Danish banks currently constitute and that we are opposed to fragmentation. We are for a number of reasons. I have explained here some specific drawbacks, and so we also have a fundamental reluctance to go in and set limits for private companies and start to forcibly split them up. We think that there is something fundamental about this, and we do not want to be part of it as a bourgeois government.

This is not a situation we have to face again, and that is why we have taken numerous initiatives both in the Folketing and at European level to ensure that the financial sector is much better equipped and upholstered, a new financial crisis had to come up again. Because we do not want there to be uncertainty about the financial sector. There must be a high level of confidence, because we need this as citizens and as consumers. Businesses need this, investors need it, they need it abroad, so there is confidence that we have a robust and efficient financial sector in Denmark. I believe that we carried out six bank packages from 2008 to 2013, and the sixth banking package dealt with precisely that around the SIFIs, which, of course, ensures stricter requirements for large Danish financial and mortgage institutions with tightened liquidity and capital requirements, i.e. fundamentally better upholstery. If you just have to take a concrete approach to the resolution that you have to divide up the financial sector, you can look up the report by the Rangvid Committee and see what they are writing about whether there should be a split of the SIFIs and the report says: "It would not have changed the course of the financial crisis in Denmark if large financial institutions, including SIFIs, had split up into banks, mortgage and insurance, or a split of the financial institution's part into retail and investment parts, which, among other things, had occurred. recommended by the so-called Liikanen report. In this context, it should be noted that the investment part constitutes a relatively small part of the total activity of Danish financial institutions. So this is not an issue that has not been assessed by the expert body, and that is why it is perhaps also the wrong place to go to say: if you had made a division, the world would be a better place. Basically, I don't believe that. But the problem has been that we have simply not ensured that the financial sector has been upholstered enough to be able to counter a major shock to the economy. And that is also why we are now doing stress tests on financial companies to see if even in extreme situations someone would be at risk. And if that is the case, the requirements for upholstery will be tightened up. So, for many reasons, the Left cannot support the resolution. And I have promised to welcome the Conservative People's Party, which does not support the proposal either.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425135037	Thank you to the Enhedslisten for raising this discussion today by making this proposal to set up a structural commission to assess the possibilities for splitting systemically important financial institutions. In short, what are some financial supermarkets we have today and must they be split up and in what way? In fact, this is a proposal that we also had in the SF, where a year ago we came up with a proposal about splitting up these financial supermarkets, and it is completely in line with the Enhedslisten's proposal, so I might as well start by saying that we will also support this resolution. Then why? Yes, I believe I heard the Liberal Alliance rapporteur say that this is approaching expropriation. I think that is a big misunderstanding about what we are talking about. Because there is actually a long and handsome liberal legacy, especially in the United States, about anti-trust, about looking at how to make a market work well, especially if there are market-dominant players if there are competition problems. It extends from Woodrow Wilson to Franklin D. Roosevelt, who said so much that we cannot have any players who become so big that they are a nuisance to the market; they outcompete others, and that is bad for the products, and in the end it is also a threat to democracy. That is why Standard Oil, one of the largest oil companies, was split up, it was not taken over, it was not expropriated, but it was divided into different parts that could then compete against each other. That was also why AT&T, the American telephone company, was split up when Graham Bell, who invented the phone, was suddenly sitting on the entire telephone market. Still, it seemed to be the rough side. So it was liberals who said: when we see clear market failures, for the sake of consumers, we will have to go in and correct them in order to have a better functioning market. We have got financial supermarkets in Denmark to such an extent that I really think it is necessary to call a shotgun, and I am glad that Enhedslisten is also fighting this issue, because it is a problem when we have huge large banks, which are also sitting on mortgages and which are also sitting on insurance, pensions, etc., and now a new door has even been opened for new ones, what should I say, more or less creative tools with credit transfers and other things. And in this way, it is necessary to look at this whole area. As I mentioned before, one can almost call the advantages of an SIFI bank, one of the very large banks, a cheek egg. Firstly, they are told that they are too big to fail, it is a state guarantee that they cannot go down. It leads to what is called moral hazard, as Mr. Pelle Dragsted also mentioned that it is a recognised concept, since if there are no clear consequences that match the actions you take, it could potentially lead to deeply irresponsible banking. And that's why it's dangerous to have banks that are too big to fail. Margrethe Vestager and many others have also said that we should not have it. The second part of the cheek egg is, of course, that we get indirect state aid for the largest banks with a value of up to 17 billion. kr. Every year it is Kraka who has done the analysis because when you get the SIFI stamp, you also get cheaper interest rates on the market because you get a better rating, and it is therefore money that burdens the state budget, so to speak, or the state balance. So it is an indirect state aid that has a value of up to 17 billion. kr. The third thing about the SIFIs is that you have to choose these internal models, the so-called IRB models, in relation to the standard models. It may sound very technical, but it means that we really do not know if there are so much tougher capital requirements for SIFIs than there are for other banks, because you calculate it completely differently. As some banks went from being ordinary banks to getting the SIFI stamp, it could actually be seen that the difference was 9 percentage points. Well, it's a pretty significant difference from the whole discussion that you say: Well, there are tough demands, there are significantly tougher capital requirements, and then you have to look at that in the big picture. Unfortunately, my speaking time has run out, but I just want to say that I think this is really, really important and that, of course, the SF supports the proposal, which is all about clarifying this problem and coming up with a model. I can't believe there's no more support for that. Thank you.

It is very natural, and it is not something that the left has come up with, but it is a concept that is included in most economic textbooks. At the same time, the merger of various parts of the financial sector, as we have seen, has also increased the risk of a crisis in one part of the financial sector, for example. The United States, which has more powerful anti-trust legislation than we have here, and moreover has such legislation that prevents them from reaching that level. Magnus Barsøe, a debate editor at Finans.dk, wrote in a very fine column: Would we accept if there were only three hairdressers in Denmark? And what do we think it would mean for the price of a haircut if we had three hairdressers to choose from? In fact, that is what is really the situation in our mortgage. Now Nykredit, as far as I can see, has also taken over DLR Credit, so the concentration has probably increased from 80%. For maybe 85 or 90 percent, without putting your head on the block. Because of this, because of the problems with this moral hazard, with the threat to competition and the problem of allowing banks to become so big, we have also historically had limitations on the size of banks. After the crisis of the 1930s, people were put in a lot of trouble all over the world. In the United States we know the famous Glass-Steagall legislation, but here in Denmark we had very clear fire walls between, for example. banks and mortgage banks. You just couldn't run a mortgage if you were a financial institution. This also meant that we had a very long period of quite great stability in the financial sector from the 1930s until the 1980s, and perhaps it was the absence of crises that gave rise to a period, the so-called neoliberal era, in which the ties were loosened in earnest and among other things. removed the firewalls between mortgages and banks, which led to the transformation of the old Danish mortgage associations, which we have enjoyed very much, into public limited companies and incorporated into the large financial supermarkets, most of them at least. Perhaps, after the financial crisis that struck us a few years ago, we could have expected a similar willingness to intervene and to ensure that the banks were split up again, but that has not happened, at least not in Denmark. In the European Union there was a commission led by the Finnish National Bank Director Erkki Liikanen, who in fact, back in 2010, I think, proposed that we should look at dividing up the largest European banks. In Britain, it was taken up and implemented what is called ring-fencing, where the customer-oriented part of the banks is fenced, so that the more speculative part of the banks can be dropped without harming ordinary people, and in the United States, for example, we have. a limitation on the proportion of deposits that individual banks may have. In Denmark we did almost the opposite. There we went in and said that we have some banks that we are giving this SIFI stamp, that is, which are important institutions that we cannot let fall, and that is to say that we recognised that we have banks that are too big to fail or are too big to fail, and we live with that, and then we make some harder demands on them. The problem is, as Mrs Lisbeth Bech Poulsen also mentioned, that it has given these banks some competitive advantages. In fact, you reward them for being so big. They can find themselves easier because they have this SIFI stamp. It is clear that it lowers the risk when you know that the state comes and rescues you so that they can borrow money cheaper than other banks. This also means, of course, that they will be better placed to compete against smaller banks. So there are a lot of good reasons to look at this. There have been some objections during the debate. There has been talk that it would be an expression of expropriation. I do not understand that, too. Having anti-cartel legislation, ensuring that there is real competition and that there is no total market dominance for individual companies is an integral part of the regulation of any capitalist society. The question is, of course, where and when you do it, but it has nothing to do with expropriation. On the contrary, it actually has something to do with protecting smaller companies from unfair competition from large ones. In this sense, it is also a little interesting that one of the chief economists in CEPOS, Otto Brøns-Petersen, wrote a column not so long ago in which he actually pleaded that it might well be that one should start to look at whether it is wise that one has banks that are so large, because it requires such extensive regulation when we have such large banks, and that it might be wiser to go the opposite way and make banks smaller, but in return let them take care of themselves. It is of course a little interesting, according to the speech of the Liberal Alliance rapporteur up here, that there are some small cracks in the ranks of liberals when it comes to this issue. But with those words, I can hear that it will not be today that we are setting up this working group, and that is a little too bad, because it was certainly a way of getting one more, what can be said, in-depth clarification of the issues that we are discussing today. Nevertheless, I would, of course, like to thank you for the debate and for the fact that, in these years, we in Denmark have a serious debate about our financial sector and how we make it work for the benefit of the general public. We do not agree on it all, but I think it is a good thing that we have an enlightened and thorough debate on those issues, and I also think that I would like it to be, this is my last chance to say so, as I am on my way out, and I would like to congratulate our Minister for Business for having taken an active part in this debate during the period he has been Minister. I think that's been really good. Compared to what we have seen in the past from other ministers, without mentioning names, I think it has been positive, and I think it has helped to lift the debate in this area on the financial sector, because it is something that concerns many Danes. It may be complicated, but it affects all of our lives. So thank you for that.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425141010	Thank you. With this motion for a resolution, the Radical Left is proposing that by the end of 2019 the government put forward legislative proposals to abolish the requirement for financial security in cases of spouse reunification. The Radical Left states that recovery is rarely carried out in the security and that the claim is superfluous and affects Danes who want marriage. It may not come as a great surprise when I say that the government cannot support this resolution. It is the government's view that it is both appropriate and reasonable for us to make such a demand. And unlike the Radical Left, the government does not think that the requirement for security is superfluous. The requirement for financial security implies that resident foreigners and Danes who wish to have a spouse in this country should normally provide an economic security of DKK 100,000. for a period of 10 years. Precisely because the government believes that it is important to ensure that the family partner does not place the public burden on it, the amount was raised from DKK 50,000 in the summer of 2018. to the present 100,000. The draft law was adopted by a broad majority here in Parliament. This appropriation is intended to cover any public expenditure on assistance under the Act on Active Social Policy or the Integration Act which may subsequently be paid to the foreigner applying for spouse reunification. If such assistance is provided, the municipality must reclaim the amount of the security paid and the requirement for financial security thus helps to create an incentive for the couple to become self-supporting and thus become an active part of the society to which they have now come. In addition, the scheme provides for a reduction in the amount of the security provided that the couple passes Danish examinations. It must be said that this motivates the spouse to learn Danish precisely, and the requirement ensures that the municipalities can be reimbursed for support paid to foreigners who are brought together in this country. The Radical Left believes that the requirement for security is superfluous because the ongoing dependency requirement is a sufficient incentive for spouses not to apply for support. I have to say, I totally disagree with that. The requirement for a financial security and the ongoing credit claim complement each other. The maintenance requirement means that the residence permit for the spouse is subject to the condition that none of the spouses receive assistance under the Act on Active Social Policy or the Integration Act, and if one or both of the spouses or both receive such public assistance, the residence permit shall be taken into account as a starting point. However, the maintenance requirement does not allow the municipalities to have recourse to compulsory social assistance. The security, on the other hand, does. The Radical Left has questioned the municipalities' need for access to involvement, as the possibility of drawing on the security has been used in very few cases, but, as I said, it is important for the government that such an opportunity exists for the municipalities. This applies regardless of the extent of the recovery of the municipalities during a given period. The possibility of drawing on the guarantee has precisely the preventive effect that we want, and it could be that there would be more family members who would apply for support if there were no means of collecting the money. This is particularly true in a situation where the couple have been divorced, since the guarantee is still in force. The Government therefore considers that there is good reason to require financial security and, in the Government's view, it is only fair to demand that, as a spouse, you should be self-supporting in this country. We therefore only derogate from the requirement if there are very specific reasons. This applies regardless of whether the resident is a Dane or a foreigner. It is therefore the Government's view that it is both appropriate and in no way superfluous for us to demand financial security in marriage proceedings. And with that one can surely hear where it is going: the government does not support this resolution, but I do not think it comes as a surprise either for the Radical Left or others.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425144214	No, it is by no means unscriptural to ensure that legislation is designed in such a way as to ensure that people can support themselves. In practice, the ceiling will mean that there will be a great many pupils who will not get cut short the school day, but on the contrary will get their school day done longer. This is also clear from the consultation answers available. We can see that it is the Danish Teacher's Association's assessment and School and Parent's assessment and several others', that with the ceiling of how many hours can be shortened on the middle level and in the school leaving, many will have a longer school day. I also think it is a pity that, at a time when so much is being said about creating greater freedom for schools, local models are being ignored for a reduction in school days, as they have come up with in many municipalities, and which I consider to be a great satisfaction for pupils, parents and teachers alike. I think it will create quite considerable frustration among many pupils when they find that what has been mentioned is that there are now finally shorter school days, because some will mean that there will actually be a longer school day, and I would like to have clarified in committee how many pupils it is that actually gets a longer school day. I think it would be very important to clarify that. Now that there is so much talk about how good it is that it gets shorter, it was actually really good to know how many students get a longer school day out of the bill here. We think that we should listen to the criticism that lies in a large number of the hearings so that the ceiling over the two hours set out in the new section 16b is dropped, and we intend to table an amendment to the legislative procedure to remove the ceiling of the legislation.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425163053	The debate is open and the first to speak is the Danish Minister for Culture.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425163122	With motion for a resolution B 114, the authors of the Danish People's Party wish to require the government to submit a legislative proposal to clarify the law on the collection of children's certificates in connection with the recruitment of staff, etc., so that it is clear from the law that the child's certificate must contain information on certain sex crimes committed against children under 15 years of age in the last 10 years in order to be valid, provided that, for persons who have not yet reached the age of 25, a certificate which dates back to the 15th year of the person is sufficient, provided that he has resided in Denmark for the last 10 years. First of all, let me stress that the government naturally shares the intention to protect our children from sexual abuse. Like the proposers, the government is very concerned that our children and young people can travel safely both in schools and institutions and in their leisure activities. The children's certificate is a very good tool because it contributes to the overall information that the recruiting authority has about the person considering hiring or associating as a volunteer. The child certificate is a good tool. And it is important that we continue to look at whether the children's certificate rules have been set up well enough. We did so by 2018 at the latest, where we made a change of announcement to avoid any doubt about companies on e.g. The film area also has to obtain children's certificates. However, the rules need to make sense, and we should not tighten just to tighten, let alone if this means that we will introduce barriers with new rules that create some other major problems. And I believe that the balance has been tipped off in the proposal from the Danish People's Party. The proposal corresponds in substance to the motion for a resolution tabled by the Danish People's Party B 72 in February 2016 and its amendments. The proposal with amendments was then rejected by all parties of the Folketing, with the exception of the Danish People's Party. What is new from the proposal discussed in 2016 is that the proposers have taken into account from the outset the consequences of the wording of B 72, which cut off all persons under 25 years of age from working with children and young people under 15 years of age. The concerns raised by the government in dealing with B 72 have been and now three years have passed. The Government continues to believe that the proposal will have far-reaching negative consequences for employment in the field of children and the number of volunteers in sport and association. This applies both to young Danes and to integrating refugees and family members into Danish society, so that they can understand Danish values. As the rules are today, the Children's Certificate Act makes it compulsory for authorities, companies and associations to obtain a children's certificate when employing or employing a person who must have direct contact with children under the age of 15. The scheme covers both volunteers and employees, including educators and pedagogues in day care institutions and leisure schemes, day care providers, foster families, staff at 24-hour institutions and social pedagogical residences, teachers and staff in schools with pupils under the age of 15, lifeguards in swimming halls, coaches and scout managers. The problems with the motion for a resolution from the Danish People's Party are as follows: the requirement that a child certificate should go back 10 years in time to be valid will, as a rule, mean that no one who has not lived in Denmark for the last 10 years, regardless of nationality, can be employed in a post involving contact with children under the age of 15. It will also include Danes who have lived abroad for a period within the last 10 years. Although the proposal allows Danes who have resided abroad, or foreign nationals who come to Denmark, to present a corresponding certificate from their country of origin and thus comply with the Danish rules, it does not solve the problem. In addition, there may be fundamental differences between criminal offences committed by countries. So what will be punishable in Denmark will not necessarily be punishable in other countries. A child certificate can never stand alone, nor has it ever been the intention of child certificates. There must always be an overall assessment of a person, whether it is an authority that hires a person for a kindergarten, an association that engages a volunteer, or a company that hires a person to deal with children. Therefore, in connection with the treatment of B 72, the government went into dialogue with the National Association of Municipalities, which in the autumn of 2016 issued inspirational material to the municipalities for the recruitment of people with shorter stays in Denmark. The inspirational material contains procedures and attention points based on experience and dialogue with a number of municipalities, which make company-oriented offers for e.g.: refugees, family members, EU citizens and Danes who have been living abroad for a number of years. It is precisely through the dissemination of the good examples that we already have that we protect children and young people as best we can, but also at the same time ensure the recruitment and employment of competent staff in the field of children and in society, which is so vital to our society. All in all, the government considers that the proposal is not the right way to address this very important issue, i.e. how we can best ensure that there is no sexual abuse against our children. On the contrary, the proposal creates some fundamental problems with how our voluntary association life can function and with integrating foreigners into our society in relation to our values. That is why the government cannot support the motion for a resolution. The government, of course, recognises and is pleased that the municipalities and sports and associations have already found good solutions to solve the problem. And that is why, in conclusion, I would like to stress once again that a child certificate should never be left alone. The child attestation is a tool that complements the employer's organisation of work, including how best to ensure the best interests of children.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425171012	Thank you, President. ParlaMint-DK_20190425171521	The debate is open. The Secretary of State.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425171535	Thank you very much. I would like to start by thanking Mr. Holger K. Nielsen's motion for a resolution B 124 which will require the government to introduce a principle of a minimum parliamentary membership of two-thirds majority for Danish participation in war, even if I will wait to conclude that I do not agree with the proposal. The government has read the proposal with great interest. Decisions to send Danish women and men to the hot spots of the world are among the heaviest that we take as politicians. That is why the government also supports the fundamental desire to seek as broad parliamentary support as possible for military broadcasts. It is therefore also a principle that the government has actually followed in practice during this period of government. However, the Government considers that there are a number of concrete elements in the resolution which make it impossible for us to support the specific proposal. Firstly, accepting the proposal would override a very important principle, namely that it is the prerogative of the current government to conduct its foreign and security policy at all times. In accordance with Paragraph 19 of the Constitution, the current government must: 2. obtain the consent of the Folketing when it comes to using military means of power. But we think that consent will, in principle, have to be obtained by a simple majority in the Folketing. In addition, the government considers that the proposal runs the risk of watering down the principle of qualified majority voting in the Folketing, which, moreover, is reserved in the Constitution for exceptional constitutional decisions on the surrender of sovereignty under Article 20 of the Constitution. Secondly, the proposal argues that a two-thirds majority principle is necessary in order to promote transparency. Furthermore, the motion for a resolution states that a principle of qualified majority voting in connection with Danish participation in war will mean that information and background knowledge will be discussed in a wider democratic forum than the narrowest possible. The motion for a resolution thus equates the principle of qualified majority voting with greater openness and sharing of information. The government does not agree with that. We are convinced that openness and information sharing are created through good and close cooperation with the Danish Parliament. It was also one of the key recommendations of the historical investigation which the government, together with the Folketing, is following up and which we have discussed. It is worth remembering that the Constitution already ensures today that decisions on Danish participation in armed conflicts are subject to a particularly thorough democratic discussion in the Folketing and in the Foreign Policy Name. Motions for resolutions on military contributions are thus discussed both in confidence in the Name and openly among the elected parliamentarians in the Folketing, the widest democratic forum in Denmark. There is therefore nothing in the present system that applies under the Constitution that prevents an in-depth, open and wide discussion of decisions on military contributions. At the same time, there is nothing in Mr. Holger K. Nielsen's motion for a resolution which, in the view of the government, will further promote openness. Thirdly, the motion for a resolution is unclear with regard to the qualified majority. It is not clear whether it is a qualified majority of all 179 members of the Folketing, which is in fact the principle used in voting on the surrender of sovereignty, or only a qualified majority of the members present. The fundamental desire for broad parliamentary support for war participation is a principle that the government has already implemented in practice, and we can be proud of that. But because of the elements just mentioned in the proposal, the government cannot support the resolution before us. However, I would like to stress once again that the government's agreement is in relation to the spirit of the proposal, and so I would like to thank Mr Seligman. Holger K. Nielsen for the presentation and also a thank you for the debate that I look forward to.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425174248	Thank you. I have to start by saying that I pulled the release number, but unfortunately I didn't take the release number today. Denmark is one of the countries that often contribute to international missions, including where it is dangerous, for example in Afghanistan. We have therefore committed ourselves to being extra thorough in preparing such far-reaching decisions, since with a parliamentary decision we potentially endanger the lives of Danish soldiers. That is why it is also an important debate which the SF has raised, although the Left does not agree with the resolution, to which I shall return. Nevertheless, it is extremely important to discuss how we deal with issues relating to the participation of Danish soldiers in international missions. In recent years, however, we have built up some experience in the field both in relation to soldiers sent out, but also in relation to how to handle returned soldiers with scars on their bodies and souls. The subject has again become topical after the analysis of Danish war participation, which was published in February, a comprehensive investigation. In relation to the resolution itself, the Left sees a number of problems, although we have great sympathy for the idea behind it, namely that we must ensure as many Danes, as many Members of Parliament as possible, behind a decision to go to war. According to the Constitution, it is the government's prerogative to conduct Denmark's foreign and security policy. By introducing a requirement for a qualified majority, that principle may be watered down. A qualified majority is used in the exercise of sovereignty under Article 20 of the Constitution. It is the view of the Left that the use of qualified majority voting should continue to be reserved for these special cases. I am not, when I read the proposal here, quite a hundred percent sure of what it is you want. You want the Folketing to impose a two-thirds majority principle on the government, is it making a law? I heard earlier that it was not to adopt a law but to lay down a principle, as was said, in a political agreement. I suppose that's what you mean by an internal political agreement that the government should stand up to in this area. I'm not quite sure, though I don't doubt Mr. Søren Søndergaard's historical references to the EU that such an agreement is in accordance with the Constitution, but it is clear that it then binds politically the parties that have signed it. But that is something that has been described in the Constitution in the places where qualified majority voting is required, and that is not the case with participation in wars. What the proposers intend to do with the proposal is to give more openness to the decision to send Danish soldiers to war. As I said, this is an honourable ambition, but the question is whether the use of qualified majority voting will contribute to it. For the Left, it is difficult to see how a qualified majority will bring more openness into the process. On the other hand, good and confidential cooperation between government and parliament is important. If necessary, efforts can be made to strengthen this, but a qualified majority will make no difference in this respect as regards transparency. The decision to send Danish soldiers to war differs from the other decisions taken by the Folketing. Such decisions are discussed in confidence both in the Foreign Policy Name, which the Foreign Minister mentioned, and in full public opinion in the Folketing, so that all citizens can actually follow the parliamentary process. For the Left, it is important that there is as broad consensus as possible when Danish soldiers are deployed for armed missions. It gives peace to the soldiers' work both during and after the mission. However, it is not the Left's view that the use of a qualified majority would be beneficial in this respect, and therefore the Left cannot support the resolution.

I would like to say that I think this is an important proposal because it is a major decision to go to war. It is a great decision for the soldiers who are being sent out; it has been described. So sending people to war, sending the country to war, is a big decision. But it is also a big decision in that it is not something that can just be changed. I mean, it's not like you can just go into another country one day, take part in an invasion and then hold a parliamentary election and then say: well, then we pull out again. So, everyone knows that the smallest part of a war is to start it, it is to start it, whereas the biggest part of the problem of a war is to get out again. Firstly, once you have gone in, you have changed the situation on the ground; secondly, you have assumed a great responsibility for all those with whom you cooperate, which means that entering is something that binds governments at many stages in the future in relation to their freedom of action, regardless of how the voters vote, regardless of whether the voters want to stop it. We can only see the problems of getting out of Afghanistan as a very illustrative example of that. And that is where I think transparency requires more than this proposal, and I think we should come back to that. However, as I said, a majority is important. That is why we can support the proposal. Then it's the question, what exactly does qualified mean. Should it be two thirds, should it be something else, what should the precise term be? So this is a motion for a resolution, which is why I assume that what we are going to be using this motion for a resolution is to continue to work, and if we adopt it in this form, then we will have to draw up a specific draft agreement which will then be the subject of negotiations between the parties of the Folketing, and we will then be able to resolve any problems that may be discussed in this context.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425181540	Thank you for the debate and also thank you for the nice words that have fallen over me. I'm glad. As I have said before, it was an epoch-making decision that was made here in the Danish Parliament when we decided to conduct what Anders Fogh Rasmussen called an activist foreign policy. It really meant militaristic foreign policy, and in subsequent years Danish foreign policy became enormously militaristic. It was activist before Anders Fogh Rasmussen; it was also activist in the 1990s, in the 1980s, in the 1970s. Denmark has thus always pursued an activist foreign policy. But that was the hugely narrow majority in 2003, which I believe made a great impression on us. We had a long debate; we stood here all day during the third reading and repeatedly made clear that it was SF, it was Enhedslisten, it was the Radical Left, and it was the Social Democrats that it was unsustainable that the Prime Minister wanted to use this small majority. It was completely untenable because the Danish soldiers are entitled to have a broad majority behind them. We sent them out, and we didn't know then how violent it would be. We were some who warned against it and said: we are quite sure that soldiers will come home in body bags. Not everyone was fully aware that this could be the consequence of it. But that was the consequence of it. I believe that when all countries make major decisions, they really must be aware that they do not run things through by a narrow majority. And I have told various minorities around the world: If you want to separate yourselves, you must have a broad majority behind you; you must have a great legitimacy behind you. I've told the Catalans, and I've told the Faroese. I have said: it is not acceptable for you to try to detach by a small majority. I have said to the British, to the Brexitians: if you want to get out, you have to get a broad majority behind the decision to be made, the agreement to be made. And that is so wrong that it has not been done in Britain. It was, as I said earlier, my own starting point after the 1992 referendum that we could not run through a hard line with such a small majority as was the case there, it would divide the population completely for many years to come. It just cannot be done, and that is why we need to think in a broad majority. I know that people are trying to get a broad majority when we are going to have military operations, and all respect for that is good enough. He didn't give a shit about the consequences of it, he didn't care if there was a UN mandate; he just wanted to go. And at an early stage, he would do so at an early stage. So you might ask yourself: would a two-thirds majority have prevented it? No, it wouldn't. I totally agree with that. And that would not have prevented bad decisions, nor necessarily increased openness, but it would have ensured that Anders Fogh Rasmussen would have had to negotiate with the Social Democrats, at least as they had done before the final decision. There were long negotiations between Per Stig Møller and Mogens Lykketoft and Niels Helveg Petersen, but they ran out into the sand. And that would have secured a fresh reflection on this whole project. So I definitely think it would have had an impact. But I also agree with all those who say that it is not a security against irresponsible decisions. Sir. It may well be that there is one or two who are, but in general it has been the clear position of all governments, of all the defence ministers, of the army leadership, which I have also discussed these things a great deal with that it is bad if there is too narrow a majority; if there is too much uncertainty afterwards; if there is too much controversy about what people are doing. You can't prevent that from happening, but it'll give you greater security if there's a broad majority behind it. And that's what this is all about. As I said, it is very much derived from the bad Iraq decision in 2003 that historians have explained in detail in the report they have produced. We believe that this will give some sort of brake block if there are prime ministers or parliamentary majority who are in the process of doing something stupid, dragging Denmark into something that is unwise. ParlaMint-DK_20190425182950	We are opening the debate, and we are opening the debate with the Minister of Justice. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425183010	Thank you. The Enhedslisten and the Alternative have tabled a motion for a resolution requiring the government to amend the current guidelines on the place of birth of its passport and driving licence. The proposers believe that the individual should be able to determine more clearly the place of birth of the person's passport or driving licence, so that the place of birth can be indicated, regardless of the current official name of the place of birth. Before I come to the government's position on the proposal, I would like briefly to try to explain how the rules have been tightened up today and the considerations behind it. According to passport legislation, the National Police can lay down guidelines on the handling of passport applications by municipalities. The guidelines show, among other things, that information about the birth place of the passport applicant is automatically retrieved from the birth place field of the Central Register of Persons, what we call the CPR in daily speech. This scheme was introduced on 1 January 2012 at the request of the National Police. When the scheme was amended, it was first to provide information on the place of birth of the same person across different systems and, secondly, to reduce the risk of manual errors. Where it is important for the information to be given in the same way across different systems, this is due to the fact that otherwise doubts may arise as to the accuracy of the information. There may also be doubts as to whether the citizen is the person who he or she pretends to be. Exceptionally, it remains possible to indicate a place of birth in the passport which is different from the information in the field of birth registration in the CPR. This may happen if the municipality considers that the indication of a particular place of birth may result in the person not being able to travel or risk being detained abroad. As far as driving licences are concerned, it is basically the same thing. However, there is the difference that the birthplace of persons born abroad is not automatically indicated on the driving licence itself. The place of birth shall instead be indicated on the driving licence by the municipality with manual entry based on the place of birth which has been automatically transferred to the driving licence register from the CPR. Work is currently under way on the technical implementation of the procedure in the field of driving licences in order to avoid manual entry. The place of birth indicated by the passport and driving licence is therefore deducted from the CPR. People born abroad will, as a starting point of the CPR, register the country where the birth took place. The name of the place of birth registration in the CPR is the current name at the time of registration of the place where the birth registration authority is located. So it is not the name of the place that applied at the time of birth. Then it is the case that, according to the practice which currently applies to the indication of the place of birth registration in the CPR, a country must be recognised as the State of Denmark in order to be able to be designated as the place of birth registration. For a person such as: was born in one of the Palestinian territories, the municipality may, at the request of the person concerned, instead offer to register in the CPR the Middle East in the field of birth registration if the person concerned does not want Israel to appear. It will then be transferred to the passport and driver's license. It was such an attempt to explain how the systems are practically connected. With regard to the government's position on the resolution, I would like to begin by saying that this is not the first time that the issue of the place of birth registration has been discussed in the Folketing. It's turning into a classic, I think. As I recall, in my time at least I have experienced oral questions, consultations, questions and now the motion for a resolution on the subject by the same authors, so we must pay tribute to the persistence of the authors of the motions for resolutions. The Government continues to believe that there should be no change to the current guidelines on the indication of the place of birth in Danish passports and driving licences. In other words, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution. In this context, it is important to bear in mind what the purpose of a passport is, firstly, that the authorities can ensure the identity of the person using the passport and, secondly, that the information contained in the passport is correct and accurate. The National Police have stated that it is their professional assessment that it will be associated with a number of disadvantages to introduce additional options in relation to the location of birth registration separately on the territory. Among other things: such a scheme would mean that the place of birth registration is not identical in passports and in CPR. The National Police have also stated that with the support of today's system, a change in the current system will mean that the passport manufacturer, a private company, will have to make changes manually. It considers the National Police to be inappropriate, as for security reasons it is aimed at limiting as much as possible the need for people to manually change information in an authority document. At the same time, the National Police estimate that manual updates will increase the risk of error detection. Driving licences are also widely used in a Danish context as a genuine identification document, and therefore the same conditions apply with regard to driving licences. To sum up briefly, it is essential for me, as Minister of Justice, that our credentials are secure and to be trusted. This assumes that the information is the same across systems and documents and that we limit the risk of manual errors as much as possible. When this is important, it is related to the fact that otherwise there may be doubts as to the accuracy of the information and whether the citizen is also the person who the citizen is pretending to be. I recognise that this is an issue that may arouse strong feelings among some, but in balancing considerations, the security of our documents of identification and their use to me as Minister of Justice is now more important on this point. Thank you.

Rasmus Nordqvist has written the following: Land borders move historically, nations arise and disappear, and we people attach great personal and political importance to geography and belonging.
