Sweden. As it is today, there is no common thread in the Danish authorities' handling of the issue. As Information writes today: For Danes born in Taiwan, it is allowed to have this country registered as the place of birth, although Denmark does not recognise Taiwan as an independent country. The case is not new, and there have been a number of consultations and questions about this case. But the answers have not been very clear, and it has most often worked as bureaucracy for the sake of bureaucracy in our opinion. We note that there are different practices in other European countries and that many others are much more flexible than the Danish authorities. Since the nationality of the place of birth has no practical or security justification, the Alternative and Enhedslisten propose that Danish authorities be given the opportunity to soften up the current inconsistent rules and accommodate Danish citizens for whom this is a very personal and important identity issue. We imagine that Denmark can introduce rules that are as flexible as in Sweden. Under the heading "place of birth', the place where the person was born is first written and then the country where the place belonged when the person was born. Upon application, the person may choose whether he or she will have both place and country with or only the place. I should like to welcome the Radical Left and say that they support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425191326	The debate is open. The Minister of Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20190425191341	I would like to begin by thanking the SF for having tabled the motion for a resolution, which obliges the government to table a bill before the end of the year, which tightens the penalty for threats from 2 to 4 years, if the threat is due to the victim's attachment to or apostasy from a religion, a community of opinion or a political conviction, or if the threat is intended to prevent the victim from using his freedom of expression. The motion for a resolution also instructs the government to develop a plan for the administration of victims of threats and to set up a central research and investigation unit in this area. This is not the first time that we in the Danish Parliament have discussed the framework and general conditions of freedom of expression in Denmark, and there is good reason for this, because the framework for the expression of freedom of expression is a highly topical issue at a time when social media have meant that more and more citizens have the opportunity to participate in the public debate. Freedom of expression is something that we must safeguard, and the government, like the proposers, is concerned about ensuring this. Freedom of expression is a fundamental prerequisite for a well-functioning democracy and forms the basis for a free political debate. Freedom of expression ensures that attitudes can be exchanged and that, as a society and as individuals, we continue to develop. That is why I also fully agree with the authors of the motion that threats to freedom of expression are deeply offensive, and I believe basically that we should all be able to participate in the public debate without the risk of being subsequently faced with threats. Views must meet with other views and arguments, not with threats of violence. The threats and gross hoaxes are never the answer, and unfortunately threats and gross hoaxes can help to dampen the desire to participate in the public debate. The Government has therefore already taken a number of steps in this area. Most recently, the penalty has been lifted for threats to people in public service who express themselves in the public debate. It should also be noted that it is already an aggravating circumstance under the criminal code if a person is subject to threats or the like due to his or her legal statements in the public debate. It is because threats based on a person's legitimate statements in the public debate can prevent others from taking part in the debate. Precisely because freedom of expression is very much at the heart of this government, we have chosen to set up a Commission on Freedom of expression. After all, the Freedom of Speech Commission is currently working on identifying and assessing the conditions and development of freedom of expression in Denmark, and as part of the work of the Commission is being carried out, among other things. a larger population survey. The study will be wide-ranging, and among the themes are among others: fear of speech and self-censorship and the culture of debate on social media. Therefore, we will all be wiser on these issues when the Freedom of expression commission gives its report later this year. I would like to emphasise that the government is always ready to see whether more can be done to support freedom of expression, and I have great sympathy for the intention behind this proposal. I just think that it should be made clear whether the measures proposed are what is needed or whether something else or more needs to be done, and it is with this background that the government cannot support the resolution. We simply want to wait for the report from the Freedom of Freedom Commission, where we will have the big, wide debate on it. At the same time, I would like to stress that the government will go openly to further discussions with the Danish Parliament on this important and relevant issue and will also look forward, as I have said, to receiving the initiative of the Commission on Freedom of expression for the debate.

ParlaMint-DK_20190425194724	First of all, I would like to thank the SF, which, with this resolution, contributes to a very good and important debate on the fight against trafficking in human beings and the gross exploitation of vulnerable workers. The motion for a resolution instructs the government to submit before the end of the year a bill amending the criminal code on trafficking in human beings. Today it is the case that it is a condition for being convicted of trafficking in human beings for forced labour that people have been forced to work or have not really had another choice. The motion for a resolution proposes to change this to extend the provision to work which, without forced labour, contains elements of gross exploitation and inhumane treatment. It has not been long since we last discussed the fight against the exploitation of vulnerable people to work in very miserable conditions. In March, together with the Minister for Employment, I was in consultation on this very subject, and I actually see the proposal as such in a straight line from that consultation. I think it was a very good and constructive consultation where we had good discussions and where we also touched on the idea of amending the penal code on trafficking in human beings in order to ensure that more backers are judged. As I also expressed in the conciliation, I fully agree that the fight against trafficking in human beings for forced labour and the exploitation of vulnerable people to work in very poor conditions must be strengthened, and I therefore believe that the ideas behind this resolution are not entirely biased, because I fully agree with the authors of the motion for a resolution that we should have held the backers of these matters to account. Then I am not sure that a change to the criminal code on trafficking in human beings will necessarily solve the challenges in this area. The fight against trafficking in human beings involves a great many different authorities, each of which has its own role in terms of prevention, help for victims and prosecution of backers, and that is why we need to look at it here in a comprehensive perspective to see how best we can improve our efforts. As I also mentioned at the conciliation meeting, an inter-ministerial working group has been set up as part of the government's reasonable agenda to look at possible initiatives in this area. The working group shall, inter alia: assess the need for legislative action. In this context, the working group will also have to obtain experience from other countries on, inter alia: the possibility of prosecuting the perpetrators. I have already obtained information from Belgium, to which the authors have referred in the motion for a resolution. In Belgium, as you know, they have changed their legislation on trafficking in human beings so that they have moved away from the requirement of coercion. This is certainly interesting and useful knowledge, but the Belgian labour market model is somewhat different from the Danish model, and their criminal law rules cannot necessarily be transferred directly to the Danish model. And then, as I have said, it is not certain that a change in the criminal code will solve the evidenceal challenges that we sometimes see in these cases, but these are, of course, information that I have intended and decided to continue working on, and that is why they will also be dealt with in the working group I mentioned. The working group will have to submit its recommendations as early as May. I think I also mentioned this in the consultation in March and with the recommendations of the working group, we will learn more about whether there are grounds for amending the criminal code on trafficking in human beings. I therefore think that because we are very close to May, and this is not just one thing; we should look at it broadly and we should wait for the recommendations of the working group before we decide to change the penal code in such a way, and that is why we cannot support it, even if we can see the thoughts behind it.

In addition, before the motion for a resolution was tabled at all, I had asked the Ministry of Children and Social Affairs to look at how the quality of legal certainty in the field of housing can be strengthened. And what I have asked for a proposal for is how to bring about major structural changes. I do not think it is technically or economically appropriate to set up a parallel coal excavation, as proposed in B 105, when an existing work is already under way to look at how quality and legal certainty in this area can be strengthened. Secondly, I would like to remind the parties that the government, together with the pool of rates parties back from the pool agreement last year, agreed to launch a series of initiatives aimed at improving the decision-making framework and also at improving legal certainty in cases of placement without consent. Among other things: make it a legal requirement that settings for placement without consent to children and young people's committees should include a separate assessment of parents' competences in dealing with the child and any challenges of the child. And it will be a requirement that when a psychological examination of the parents is carried out in the context of the child's examination, it should be done with an authorised psychologist. This should ensure a certain quality in the studies carried out on parental competences. Last year we adopted a large and ambitious package to strengthen the area and minimise the number of shifts and the number of crashes. Finally, I am delighted to announce that KL has today announced that they recommend the municipalities to use the so-called average model for the remuneration of foster families and that they have at the same time published a number of principles for good bargaining behaviour between municipalities and foster families. I have long been pressing for more municipalities to use the average model, and I also know that a great many of the social rapporteurs have, because we know from experience that the model reduces insecurity and conflicts between municipalities and foster families, because the model does away with the annual renegotiation of the conditions of foster families. I therefore believe that with KL's new recommendations we can take a major step towards ensuring stability and security for children placed outside the home. In addition to the fact that a number of initiatives have already been launched to strengthen quality and legal certainty in the field of housing, I do not believe that an independent expert group is the best way of digging the area. In my optics, such work should be anchored in the Ministry of Children and Social Affairs, and there are two reasons for this. First of all, the Ministry, together with the Social Security Authority, has a great deal of professional knowledge about the placement area, including knowledge about the latest research results and active efforts and the like. And it is vital to me that recommendations for strengthening the location area are based on knowledge of what works. In this regard, we have, of course, taken a major and important step in establishing the development and investment programme in the field of children, which was agreed with the 2019 Rate Pool Agreement. The development and investment programme shall strengthen the development and dissemination of effective and cost-effective interventions and practices in the children and young people field, based on methods and interventions requested in the municipalities. For me, it is absolutely crucial for strengthening the location area that future actions have a link with the knowledge-based social policy that we have launched together. Secondly, a coal excavation of the site anchored in the Ministry will also be able to draw on the Ministry's legal competences. This is, for example, important in relation to the subsequent implementation of any rule changes that could be the result of such an excavation. You have to assume that. Following on from this, I also think it is absolutely crucial that recommendations for possible legislative changes, etc. is seen in the context of existing studies of the local authorities' procedures. This applies, for example, to the annual study on the quality of municipal proceedings in the area of children, for which the government, together with the parties responsible for the 2019 Rate Pool Agreement, allocated permanent funding. It is a study to ensure that we get both a better and continuous picture of the municipalities' casework, including that we can target efforts towards municipalities that do not comply with the law. So, for these reasons, a ministerial anchoring of a possible new coal excavation of the area is therefore essential. In summary, I fully agree that there is a need to give the site a review in order to strengthen quality and legal certainty, and I would also like to congratulate the proposers for having the area on the agenda again. However, when we reject the motion for a resolution and it is a gentle rejection, it is on the basis of the two main issues I have just mentioned. We have already launched a series of initiatives for the cultivation of the area, and at the same time such work should therefore be anchored in the Ministry of Children and Social Affairs, so that the recommendations that come will be implemented so that they are based on knowledge of what works and that they are legally workable. Thank you.
